Israeli president picks Gadi Eisenkot to form government

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The anti-Netanyahu alliance led by former Israeli military chief Gadi Eisenkot has been tasked by President Isaac Herzog with forming a new government, with tricky coalition talks expected.

Israeli President Isaac Herzog has chosen Gadi Eisenkot to have the first opportunity to form a new Israeli government, according to a statement released by his office on Saturday evening.

The statement said Eisenkot was informed of Herzog's "intention to task him with forming a government" and would be invited to the president's residence, where a formal ceremony will take place on Sunday.

The decision comes after a neck-and-neck race with Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu in Israel's October election.

Eisenkot's wide-ranging group, fashioned in opposition to Netanyahu, won 48 of the Israeli parliament's 120 seats in October's elections. Netanyahu's camp secured 53, leaving neither with the required majority of 61 seats.

Former Israeli military chief Eisenkot is the leader of the centrist Yashar party and heads an anti-Netanyahu alliance.

While Netanyahu ruled out working with Arab parties to gain a majority, both Arab majority lists had announced during the week that they would recommend Eisenkot to be next prime minister.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The move is the first step toward what will almost certainly be complicated coalition talks.

Should Eisenkot succeed in forming a government, he would take over as prime minister from Netanyahu, Israel's longest-serving premier, whose religious-nationalist coalition lost its majority in the election, despite emerging as the largest bloc.

Although Eisenkot was runner up, he managed to secure endorsements from leaders of Arab parties, who are opposed to Netanyahu.

Eisenkot faces a tricky path to forming a government, involving difficult constellations of Arab and ultra-Orthodox parties. He will have 28 days to round up the 61 votes needed to form a government.

Herzog can provide a 14-day extension if Eisenkot comes up short before the mandate must be offered to someone else.

If no candidate succeeds, Israel will head to fresh elections.

Ebola: Why is there still no vaccine?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Ebola has been known about for decades, but there is still no vaccine against the Bundibugyo strain of the virus causing the current outbreak. New research raises hopes of protection against the various types of Ebola.

1976: A hitherto unknown disease breaks out near the Ebola river in the country then known as Zaire, now the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). This was the first scientifically documented Ebola epidemic.

Now, 50 years on, the DRC once again finds itself at the center of a major Ebola outbreak. This time the pathogen is the less common Bundibugyo type of the genus Orthoebolavirus. The virus was identified in 2007 during an outbreak in Bundibugyo, Uganda. Another outbreak occurred in the DRC in 2012.

How can a virus that has been known for decades continue to infect thousands of people, yet there is still no licensed vaccine or official treatment?

Torsten Feldt, a doctor of tropical medicine at Düsseldorf's University Hospital, sees this as a missed opportunity. "We had come across the Bundibugyo virus in two previous outbreaks: 2007 in Uganda and 2012 in DR Congo. So we had enough time to develop vaccines and therapeutic options. But we didn't make use of this opportunity, meaning crucial time was lost during the current outbreak."

Leonie Mayer from the German Center for Infection Research (DZIF) and the Institute for Infection Research and Vaccine Development at the University Medical Center in Hamburg-Eppendorf agrees. "They could of course have developed a vaccine and run pre-clinical trials before," Mayer told DW. She stressed, however, that their effectiveness can only be assessed and confirmed during an actual outbreak.

Mayer is the lead author of a recently published study that examined whether Ervebo, an Ebola vaccine that has already been licensed, can be used against other types of Ebola as well.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

From an immunological point of view, and compared to — for example — influenza or HIV, Ebola is a very worthwhile candidate for a vaccine. Influenza viruses are constantly mutating, and the human immunodeficiency virus is particularly good at evading our immune defenses. Ebola is far more predictable.

"I think — and perhaps it's kind of a blessing in disguise — that it will be relatively straightforward to develop a vaccine against Bundibugyo," says Mayer.

Herein lies the paradox. From a scientific point of view, Ebola is a good vaccine candidate, yet there is still no licensed form of prevention against the Bundibugyo variant that is currently circulating.

Mayer explains that vaccines cannot be developed as a precautionary measure against every potential pathogen. Lack of funding or the prioritization of other pathogens often have a role to play. Many researchers had assumed that a fresh outbreak of the Sudan Ebola virus was more likely, and vaccine development for this strain is more advanced.

Another part of the problem is that Ebola is not one single virus. The known, highly dangerous orthoebolaviruses include Ebola virus (EBOV), previously called the Zaire Ebola virus (ZEBOV), the Sudan virus (SUDV), and the Bundibugyo virus (BDBV). The existing vaccine, Ervebo, was developed and licensed for use against the Zaire variant. But there is still no approved protection against the others.

This is why the existing Ebola vaccine has been of only limited use to people in the areas of the current outbreak.

Another factor may also be relevant: The Bundibugyo virus appears to be less aggressive than the Zaire variant. At first, that sounds like good news, but it may make it harder to contain, as people remain mobile for longer after being infected, and can pass on the virus without realizing.

In the recently published study, Mayer's research team from the DZIF investigated whether the already licensed Ebola vaccine could trigger an immune response not only against EBOV, but against other types of Ebola as well. They found cross-reactive T-cell responses against Bundibugyo, and also, to a lesser extent, against the Sudan virus.

For Mayer, this is already an important finding: "We now know that the immune cells can most likely also recognize the Bundibugyo virus." But she also observes: "Our data does not enable us to answer the question of whether this immune response actually provides protection, or if it results in a milder progression of the disease." This is currently an important gap in our knowledge.

If there's a vaccine that has already been licensed, and indications that it could protect people, why isn't it simply being deployed?

Mayer explains the dilemma. For one thing, she says, it's not ethical to deploy a vaccine if you're not sure whether or how well it works. Also, if it were used on a large scale, it would no longer be possible to establish whether it actually works: A reduction in cases could also be a result of other measures, or the epidemic might already have passed its peak.

Jürgen May, a specialist in tropical diseases at the Bernhard Nocht Institute, also warns against mass vaccination: "Mass vaccination without proof of efficacy could create a false sense of security, forfeit trust, and, if it does not provide sufficient protection, make study impossible."

It's very important for there to be acceptance on the ground. "If we inoculate people with a vaccine on a large scale, and afterwards it turns out that it was nothing like as effective as we expected, it's very difficult to communicate that to people on the ground," Mayer says. In a worst-case scenario, people could subsequently refuse inoculation with more effective vaccines.

But the current outbreak also shows that medical innovation alone is not enough. Conflicts, difficult transport routes, attacks on healthcare facilities, and gaps in contact tracing all make it harder to contain.

Torsten Feldt says identifying Bundibugyo is no longer a problem in itself. What is often limiting is the practical availability of diagnostic tools. "In remote or conflict-affected regions, samples often have to be transported over long distances," he explains.

Here we see another Ebola paradox. The world is getting ever better at diagnosing infections, but it still struggles to contain outbreaks fast enough. The authors of an article in the current issue of Nature come to the same conclusion: The problem is often not identifying cases, but the speed with which people react. Finding Ebola is often easier now than stopping Ebola, they write.

Many researchers believe the current crisis could potentially also provide a great opportunity. The so-called Tumaini study, which is testing different vaccine candidates, has now been launched in the DRC.

These include Ervebo, developed by Oxford University, and a Moderna mRNA vaccine. The study aims to provide the first reliable data about their efficacy against the Bundibugyo virus.

Whether these vaccines could still help control the current outbreak is uncertain. There is hope, but experts estimate that, realistically, it will be several months before any such vaccine is available for widespread use; perhaps not until mid-2027.

"Ideally, we will develop cross-reactive pan-Ebola vaccines," Christof Geldmacher, a co-author of the DZIF study, says of their long-term plans. This is also something that is being worked on. For now, though, the focus is on developing an effective vaccine against the Bundibugyo variant of the virus.

UK cannot 'cherry pick' EU single market deal, Barnier tells BBC

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Michel Barnier says there will be 'no cherry-picking' in a deal for the UK to returns to the EU

The prime minister has said rejoining the bloc's internal market was among options he could pursue as part of a reshaping of ties since the UK's exit from the EU nearly six years ago.

But Michel Barnier told the BBC's Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg that EU members would insist on the UK readopting certain conditions, such as the free movement of people, to gain access.

He said there was not a "single market Ã  la carte" menu from which the UK could pick and choose what elements it wanted to adopt.

Barnier - who led the EU delegation during Brexit talks between 2016 and 2021, and had a three-month spell as French prime minister in 2024 - said the "door is open" within Europe to the UK rejoining as an EU member.

But he insisted that any move to rejoin the single market - which allows for goods to be freely traded without border controls - would require the UK to follow the same terms as other members.

He added that this included compliance with regulations designed to promote the circulation of goods, services, capital and people, referred to collectively within EU institutions as the "four freedoms".

"There will be no possibility to have one freedom and not the other. The four freedoms are clearly the four conditions to be inside a single market."

Since becoming prime minister in July, Burnham has suggested he could commit Labour to pursuing a much closer future relationship with Europe, including asking to rejoin the organisation as a full member, though he has ruled out major changes before the next election.

His predecessors have struck a series of deals with the EU on different aspects of how the UK and EU interact post-Brexit.

Both sides are hoping to use a meeting in Brussels in 10 days' time to unveil deals on food standards, youth visas and re-linking British industry to the EU's carbon trading regime.

Why the prime minister wants to talk about Brexit again

Of the four freedoms, the free movement of people - which allows EU citizens to live and work elsewhere in the bloc without a visa - was a key issue during the 2016 Brexit referendum.

Burnham's immediate predecessor, Sir Keir Starmer, ruled out re-establishing it for the UK in Labour's 2024 election manifesto and any move to advocate it again would prove a key dividing line with the Conservatives and Reform UK at a future poll.

Burnham recently said in an interview that he would not re-establish free movement of people without a referendum, prompting Downing Street to clarify that he was not advocating its return at this point.

He has previously said he wanted to lay out "different options" for the relationship with Europe at the Brussels summit later this month, before trying to find a consensus with the British public on a new relationship.

Barnier also predicted that EU countries would reject reinstating the British financial rebate, first negotiated in 1985, that lowered UK contributions towards the EU's annual budget while it was a member.

The UK's decision to leave the bloc had created "a new situation", he said, meaning a new rebate would be "off the table".

Barnier also compared Reform leader Nigel Farage to French politician Marine Le Pen. He said: "We have our own Farage. It is Mrs Le Pen. The same populism, the same lies, the same false promises."

Asked by Kuenssberg to clarify what he was referring to with his accusation of lies, Barnier said "for instance, when Mr Farage and Mr Johnson promised the UK people to get back [Â£350] million per week, it was not true, it was not possible. It was a lie".

The Vote Leave campaign, of which Boris Johnson became a key figure, has been criticised previously for the slogan on the side of their campaign bus that said Britain sent Â£350m a week to the EU, adding: "Let's fund our NHS instead".

Johnson said in 2017 that "once we have settled our accounts, we will take back control of roughly Â£350m per week. It would be a fine thing as many of us have pointed out if a lot of that money went on the NHS".

Critics have said that the Â£350m per week was an approximate sum which represents a "gross contribution" and did not take into account the UK's Â£75m-a-week rebate nor the EU's investments in the UK.

Johnson has since defended the claim, saying the Â£350m was based on the UK's gross contribution to the EU, and was in fact an underestimate as UK contributions were due to rise.

âIn his memoirs published in 2024, Johnson said EU money spent in the UK was not "democratically controlled by the British people", and "even if the net figure was only Â£175m, that was still a hell of a lot of money".

Farage, who led Leave UK and was not part of the Vote Leave campaign so not connected to the Â£350m claim, said: "It is the same old Barnier - arrogant, out of touch and a bully. He vindicates our decision to leave every time he opens his mouth."

At least 6 killed in shooting in Polish city of Krakow

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

A gunman who fired from a car killed at least six people, including a British tourist, in Krakow's main market square. The suspect was wounded by police and taken to a hospital.

At least six people were killed and sixteen injured in a shooting near the main market square in the southern Polish city of Krakow on Saturday.

The perpetrator, a 25-year-old Polish man who acted alone, was wounded during an exchange of fire with police, authorities said.

"Until now in Poland we haven't had such drastic cases of terrorism," Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk told reporters in Krakow.

"That's what we have to call this cruel attack on people's lives here in Krakow," he added.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

According to Krakow police, the gunman aimed at random people as he drove by the city's main square, which is a popular tourist attraction.

"At around 3 pm (1400 GMT), a man who had driven into the market square in a car began shooting at random people from inside the vehicle," police said on X, adding officers were among the wounded.

"The perpetrator was shot by police ‌officers and is currently being ‌transported ​to a hospital," police said.

Later Saturday evening, Polish police chief Marek Boron said a British citizen was among those killed.

"There are six fatalities: four women and two men; three Polish women, one British national, and two people who have not yet been identified," Boron told reporters.

According to Katarzyna Cislo, a spokesperson for the Krakow police, the shooter wore military-style fatigues and a bulletproof vest and used a long gun in the attack.

Numerous other weapons and ammunition were found in the gunman's car.

Polish Interior Minister Marcin Kierwinski said that the shooter was "fascinated by far-right ideologies" and held "very radical views."

Kierwinski also told reporters that five of the sixteen people injured in the shooting were non-Polish nationals.

He said one of those killed was a foreign national.

Polish Prime Minister Tusk posted to X that police were investigating the attack and called it a "tragic" incident.

Tusk later said Poland would observe two days of mourning for the victims of the shooting.

Polish President Karol Nawrocki wrote on X that his thoughts were with the victims and wounded, as well as their families.

The European Union's top diplomat, Kaja Kallas, said the shooting was a tragedy that will be felt far beyond Poland, adding that "Poland does not stand alone."

"I am deeply saddened by the events in Krakow. My thoughts are with the victims and their families," German Chancellor Friedrich Merz wrote on X, adding that "Germany stands alongside Poland as a friend and partner during these difficult times."

Krakow is located about 250 kilometers (155 miles) from Warsaw, the capital of Poland.

With a population of over 800,000, Krakow is Poland's second-largest city, as well as a popular tourist destination.

Houthis say at least 25 killed in Saudi air strike

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Children were among the injured people seen being carried into a nearby hospital

Media linked to the Houthi movement in Yemen say that at least 25 people, including women and children, have been killed in airstrikes by Saudi Arabia.

There has been no comment from the Saudi-led coalition backing the internationally recognised government in Yemen.

Live video footage on Al Masirah - a television station run by the Houthis - has shown rescuers pulling bodies out of the rubble of several buildings in al-Shariyah, a town to the east of the capital, Sanaa.

The bodies of young children can be seen among those dug out from the ruins.

The television station has also shown a number of injured people being carried on stretchers to hospital.

Saba news agency, which is also run by the Houthis, said the attack was carried out by Saudi Arabia.

The Saudis have led a coalition that has been fighting the Houthis for more than a decade in support of Yemen's internationally recognised government.

The war in Yemen had been dormant for several years but reignited as a result of the Iran war.

A truce began to fall apart when Saudi Arabia struck the airport in Sanaa - which is controlled by the Houthis - to prevent an Iranian plan landing there.

The Tehran-backed Houthis subsequently targeted Abha airport in Saudi Arabia, then began hitting Saudi ships that have been using the Red Sea off the coast of Yemen as an alternative oil export route to the Strait of Hormuz.

Major fighting has since resumed in Yemen between the Houthis and forces fighting for the Saudi-backed government of Yemen.

On Friday, an attack on a market in Taiz province, which has been the focus of much of the recent fighting, is reported to have killed 30 people.

The Houthis accused the Saudis of carrying out the strike, while government forces accused the Houthis of having fired a missile at the site.

The United Nations says more than 1,600 people have been killed and more than 300,000 displaced since fighting resumed.

The Saudis have regularly bombed Houthi positions, while the group has fired multiple missiles at targets inside Saudi Arabia, including airports and oil facilities.

Hours before the strike that hit al-Shariyah, the Saudi-led coalition said it intercepted a missile heading towards Riyadh.

A Houthi leader, Mahdi al-Mashat, has vowed that the group will take revenge for the latest attacks in Yemen.

US offers $20m reward for information about Iran's nuclear programme

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The US has announced a reward of up to $20 million for information about Iran's nuclear programme.

The State Department's Rewards for Justice is also offering to potentially relocate the person who provides such details.

President Donald Trump claimed last year that Iran's nuclear facilities had been "completely and totally obliterated" following a bombing campaign by the US and Israel.

Iran insists its nuclear programme is peaceful and that it has no intention of developing a nuclear weapon.

Meanwhile, the US military says it is setting up a task force to enhance Washington's efforts to restrict Iran's movement of weapons and sanctioned material across its borders.

The US military's Central Command (Centcom) said Task Force Iron Gate would operate from Tampa, Florida, and would work with other US government agencies.

As well as the movement of weapons, it will also focus on dual-use and illegal revenue streams.

Centcom estimates that its naval blockade of Iran's ports has prevented the country from exporting more than $25bn in crude oil.

Major construction ongoing near Iran missile bases, satellite images suggest

A US State Department statement about its multi-million dollar reward said it was offering the money for information about the Iranian programme, including "facilities, equipment and associated personnel".

It said Iran's "lack of compliance with the IAEA [the International Atomic Energy Agency] on safeguards obligations has led to the agency's loss of 'continuity of knowledge' regarding Iran's production and inventory of key components like [nuclear] centrifuges".

Centrifuges are needed to produce enriched uranium, which can be used to make reactor fuel for civilian use but also for nuclear weapons if enriched to around 90% purity or higher.

There had been mounting speculation that the US might resume significant strikes in Iran after the midterm elections, though Trump told reporters, prior to the polls, that he was in "no rush" to do so.

In an interview with Fox News on the eve of the midterms, the president also said the US "might need to go after Pickaxe [Mountain]", an underground site linked to Iran's nuclear programme.

This was "not a big deal" and could be done in "one hour", Trump said.

The president has repeatedly threatened to bomb the suspected underground site on previous occasions.

However, both Trump and Vice-President JD Vance have said public opposition to the war on Iran fuelled heavy losses for their Republican Party in this week's midterm elections, which has led to speculation the White House may no longer seek an escalation in the conflict.

On Friday, Trump insisted that Iran was keen to end the eight-month conflict with the US, stressing the damage to Tehran's economy and military.

"We're dealing with them now. They want to make a deal very badly," he said.

Qatar's foreign ministry spokesman said on Tuesday that both sides were "reviewing the ideas and proposals we have presented" and that it remained hopeful of a deal being agreed.

Iranian negotiators had discussed with mediators a proposal to reopen the Strait of Hormuz within two days once the US met its seven conditions.

The US and Iran reached a preliminary agreement to end the war and reopen the strait in June, but it collapsed within weeks after Iranian attacks on shipping resumed and the US reinstated its blockade.

No clear winner after tight New Zealand general election

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

New Zealand election 'too close to call' - so who's won?

There was no clear winner following New Zealand's election on Saturday, as the leaders of its two major parties preparing for negotiations to form a coalition government.

Prime Minister Christopher Luxon's centre-right National Party won 42 seats but was 19 short of the simple majority needed to form a government, despite being the largest party, preliminary results show.

Combined with their coalition partners ACT and New Zealand First, they would have a slim lead over the left-leaning bloc if a deal could be reached.

But opposition leader Chris Hipkins, whose Labour Party won 36 seats, showed no signs of conceding, saying the "shape of the next government" will remain uncertain "for a while".

The tight result followed a campaign shaped by the cost of living and unemployment.

Projections show the National Party on 42 seats and Labour on 36. The Greens are on 16, with ACT on 13, New Zealand First on 12, the Opportunity Party on 7 and Te PÄti MÄori, which represents MÄori interests, on 5.

New Zealand Prime Minister Christopher Luxon addressed supporters in Auckland as votes were being counted

The electoral commission was not due to announced the official result until 27 November.

Luxon congratulated Hipkins on a "very hard-fought race" before addressing party colleagues, saying "whatever the final count, we are incredibly proud of each and everyone for you".

He said he would run a caretaker government until the final results were confirmed later in the month, adding: "With those final results there can then be meaningful negotiations."

Elsewhere, Labour leader Kipkins said "the people have spoken", adding: "It may take a bit longer to work out what they've said."

Hipkins said the results made clear the "next parliament is going to require all of us to compromise, to work together and to find a common ground".

More than 99% of votes have been counted in New Zealand, with National and Labour neck-and-neck in terms of vote share.

But the preliminary results pointed to historically low support for the two major parties, with a combined vote share of around 60%.

They were followed by the Greens, ACT, New Zealand First, centrist party Opportunity and Te PÄti MÄori, which represents MÄori interests.

New Zealand election 2026: What you need to know

The Opportunity Party crossed the 5% threshold to enter parliament for the first time, with leader Qiulae Wong saying would work with either side.

It has been described as this election's kingmaker as it remains open to working with either major bloc, but its leaders said they would negotiate first with the party that secures the most seats.

Speaking at Opportunity's watch party, Wong told supporters: "We believe that we can change the way we do politics to focus on the future, on the next generation, and on finding common ground."

Professor Andrew Geddis from the University of Otago, an expert in election law, said "the Opportunity Party as a wildcard player" could "determine which bloc of parties is able to govern".

"It will come down to some very fine vote margins not just between the parties, but in individual electorates. We are going to have to wait for the special votes to be processed and counted over the next three weeks," he said.

Special votes are votes cast outside a voter's electorate, including overseas.

The Greens â who have proposed forcing the country's two main supermarkets to divest stores to create publicly-owned shops â saw their vote rise by a couple of percentage points.

Labour Party leader Chris Hipkins speaking to supporters

Julian McCrann, head of polling at Roy Morgan, told BBC News the cost of living was the main issue for many New Zealanders

"Three years ago, Luxon came in saying he'd be making New Zealanders' lives better and solving these economic issues, but he hasn't really delivered," he said.

More than half of the country no longer see New Zealand as a land of opportunity, according to an annual social cohesion survey. Record numbers of Kiwis have been leaving the country, with many going to Australia.

New Zealand's mixed-member proportional voting system means most governments are coalitions.

Each voter makes two choices â for the representative of their constituency in parliament and a political party.

What we know so far after Krakow gun attack

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

At least six people were killed and several others injured when a gunman suspected to have ties to the far-right opened fire in central Krakow, the second largest city in Poland.

Three bodies were found at his home when it was searched by police. Here's everything we know so far.

The attack began shortly before 15:00 local time (14:00 GMT) in Krakow's Main Market Square, the bustling central point of the city.

Police said a man drove into the square before beginning to fire "at random people from his car".

Footage from the scene shows scenes of panic as people fled, with the sound of gunfire clearly heard.

A passing tram was shot at during the apparently random attack, shattering windows but no passengers were injured.

People run for shelter as gunshots heard in Krakow

Armed officers raced to the scene and engaged in a gunfight with the attacker, who was shot within three minutes of the attack beginning, preventing a further massacre, officials said.

The gunman was shot but is alive and has been arrested.

Distressing footage authenticated by BBC Verify shows the moment a police officer confronts the suspect on the ground before firing his weapon several times.

The officer then falls to the ground, apparently having been injured himself.

Moment police officer appears to shoot Krakow suspect

Shop windows in the square have signs of damage, seemingly from bullets, with glass shards scattered along the floor.

City officials have urged people who live in the city and tourists to stay away from the area while an investigation continues.

The suspect's car, found at the intersection of Dietla and Stradomska streets, is being checked for possible explosive devices, police say. Residents of nearby buildings are being evacuated while the operation is under way.

Six people - four women and two men - are confirmed to have been killed, including a British national. Three victims are Polish and two have not been identified.

Sixteen people, including five foreigners and the attacker, were taken to hospital. Among those being treated are two police officers who were shot while detaining the attacker.

Poland's Prime Minister Donald Tusk later said three bodies - two women and one man - were found at the gunman's house when it was searched after the attack.

One of the police officers was described as being in a "very critical condition" by a Polish government minister after being shot three times while arresting the attacker. The officer is undergoing surgery, he added.

Polish authorities have not confirmed the names and ages of the other victims.

Police have identified the attacker as a 25-year-old Polish man but have not confirmed his name.

The suspect was wearing "military-style attire" and had a military-grade weapon, according to a police spokesperson. However, authorities have confirmed the attacker was not a soldier.

"Many pieces of weapons and ammunition were found in his vehicle", the spokesperson added.

Polish media has reported the suspect had a firearms permit and may have obtained the weapons from his family home.

A minister has said early investigations indicate he was inspired by "far-right ideologies". The minister also described him as an "ultra-nationalist" without giving further details.

His home is being searched with support from counter-terrorism police.

The shooting happened in one of the largest medieval squares in Europe, a busy location which is also a popular tourist destination.

Jonathan Balshaw from Ipswich was there with his Polish girlfriend Dominika. The couple were outside St Mary's Basilica, in the corner of the square, when the shooting started.

Jonathan said: "We ran down the side of the church and through the side door and hid with several other people until the gunfire died down.

"A lot of shots were fired, more than 20 with it sounding like multiple different firearms - there were two distinctly different noises."

Caitlin Shasby from Newcastle arrived in Krakow yesterday with her friend Jon for a long weekend.

She said they heard shots from their hotel room but were not sure what was happening until their hotel rang them and told them to "stay in our rooms until further notice".

Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk is on his way to Krakow and is expected to give an address later on Saturday.

Earlier, he said he was being kept informed by the interior minister and that police were investigating "the background and all the circumstances".

Krakow Mayor Åukasz GibaÅa has signed an order declaring a period of mourning in the city which will last until Wednesday, November 11.

President Karol Nawrocki said he was "deeply concerned" and was awaiting "a full explanation of the circumstances surrounding these tragic events".

US ambassador to Poland Tom Rose described it as a "savage and murderous attack".

The EU's top diplomat, Kaja Kallas, said the shooting was "a tragedy that will be felt far beyond Poland".

Lowering blood pressure after stroke or with diabetes poses challenges, in research and practice

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Liz covers cardiovascular and metabolic disease, asking why we’re not doing better or even losing ground against the world’s leading killer. Send tips on Signal at LizC.22.

CHICAGO — High blood pressure has earned its reputation as the silent killer. If someone has diabetes or has had a stroke, the risk of serious cardiovascular events posed by hypertension climbs even higher.

Three studies presented Saturday at the American Heart Association’s scientific sessions reported on recent attempts to lower blood pressure in people with these challenging medical histories, and how medical care should reach across specialties to treat them. One small but diverse trial tested a common diuretic after a stroke, another looked at intensifying treatment to reach lower-than-standard post-stroke blood pressure goals, and the third looked at going lower in people living with type 2 diabetes.

Each of the scientists leading the studies and the experts commenting on them at a media briefing emphasized the role of blood pressure. Adding a widely available drug earlier held promise for controlling blood pressure, but it was a split decision between the effects of lower numbers post-stroke and with diabetes.

“Blood pressure is defined in guidelines as the most important modifiable risk factor for recurrent stroke,” Jordana Cohen of the Perelman School of Medicine at the University of Pennsylvania, said. “We know that blood pressure control after stroke is suboptimal.”

Her trial, called REDUCE, took advantage of the common, cheap diuretic spironolactone — sometimes called a water pill. It’s usually given to people late in treatment, after three other medications haven’t worked.  But when spironolactone was given to people right after a stroke, they were more likely than those receiving usual post-stroke care to see their blood pressure drop three months later to less than 130/80 mm Hg.

Small changes are important, Cohen said about the trial, whose mission was to measure blood pressure reduction, not cardiovascular outcomes.. When blood pressure goes down by as little as 7 mm, that reduction has been associated with a 38% lower risk of recurrent stroke.

The trial was presented as an abstract and has not yet been published in a medical journal. In his comments, Bruce Ovbiagele of the UCSF Weill Institute for Neurosciences, said it’s well known that one-third of stroke patients don’t meet blood pressure guidelines, and said the study offers hope for a better approach.

“We also know that one year after a stroke has occurred, about one out of five stroke survivors have discontinued their antihypertensive medications,” he said. “This wonderful notion to try and use spironolactone is a great one because it’s a medication that’s cheap. It’s available and recognizable to so many clinicians.”

A much larger blood pressure trial in Brazil tested whether aiming for a top number of 120 mm or lower would be better than the currently recommended 140 mm.

“Almost all patients enter stroke-based antihypertensive therapy. But what we don’t know is how low should we go,” Gisele Sampaio of the Federal University of Sao Paulo in Brazil said about her OPTIMAL-STROKE study, published in the New England Journal of Medicine.

People who took medications to reach this lower systolic blood pressure goal were significantly less likely to die or have cardiovascular events within four years, compared to those who received standard treatment to lower levels below 140 mm Hg, the study found. The nonfatal events in the OPTIMAL-STROKE study included heart attack, stroke, and hospitalization for chest pain or heart failure.

Achieving these lower numbers will require medical supervision, including monitoring kidney function. There was no increase in the risk of end-stage renal disease, in the need for dialysis, or in deaths but some markers of renal function were off, Otavio Berwanger of Imperial College London and chair of the OPTIMAL study steering committee, said  about the study. But there were changes in electrolyte levels consistent with using a diuretic or how much water people drink

The related OPTIMAL-DIABETES trial, also published in the New England Journal of Medicine, came to a different conclusion.

Karla Santo of Einstein Hospital Israelita in Sao Paulo reminded her audience that the ideal blood pressure target in patients with type 2 diabetes is still uncertain. Her study sought to evaluate the lower target of less than 120 in these patients.

Its findings were neutral: Among patients with type 2 diabetes, aiming at a systolic blood pressure of less than 120 mm Hg, rather than less than 140 mm Hg, did not reduce the occurrence of major cardiovascular events or deaths.

Shawna Nesbitt of UT Southwestern Medical Center pointed out differences between men and women and between younger and older patients in these two older trials and the newer OPTIMAL-DIABETES trial. She also wondered about longer follow-up.

“I think it very strongly suggests that this trial supports rather than rebukes what we already have seen in other trials treating hypertension more aggressively in patients with diabetes,” she said. “We might begin to think about blood pressure treatment in a more nuanced way. That is to say, perhaps we’ve been lumping patients together in a way that may not necessarily always be advantageous.”

One common challenge that may arise is how to apply these results in the U.S.

“Post-stroke care is very fragmented. Neurologists take care of patients in hospital. Then patients go into primary care,” Ovbiagele said.  “So how does a nephrologist and neurologist and a primary care person manage the utilization of the medications?”

Cheryl Bushnell of Wake Forest University said doctors know what to do.

“It’s not the what, but it’s the how,” she said in her comments. “Managing blood pressure in stroke patients is incredibly difficult, I say as somebody who has to own that as a clinician. Is it the neurologist? Is it the nephrologist? Is it the primary care doctor?”

Liz covers cardiovascular and metabolic disease, asking why we’re not doing better or even losing ground against the world’s leading killer. Send tips on Signal at LizC.22.

Serie A | Roma 3-0 Sassuolo: Aquilani comfortably swept aside

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Roma made short work of sorry Sassuolo with Mario Hermoso, an own goal and Rodrigo Mora, inflicting a fifth consecutive defeat on Alberto Aquilani’s side.

The Giallorossi were still smarting after fumbling a 2-0 lead to lose 3-2 at Manchester United midweek, suffering new injuries to Pierluigi Gollini, Gianluca Mancini and Devyne Rensch, with Paulo Dybala, Mile Svilar and Lorenzo Pellegrini still out, but Manu Koné was back for the first time since his thigh injury on October 14.

Sassuolo lost four on the bounce under coach Alberto Aquilani, facing his former club for the first time here, missing Domenico Berardi, Cristian Volpato, Daniel Boloca, Ismael Koné and Fedde Leysen, so they switched to 3-5-2.

Third-choice goalkeeper Devis Vasquez had to get down and fingertip a Nemanja Matic angled drive round the far post in the opening minutes, while Duje Caleta-Car needed a goal-line clearance on the Leonardo Balerdi looping header from a Rodrigo Mora corner kick routine.

Mora’s deflected effort skimmed the crossbar, but Roma broke the deadlock when Mario Hermoso surged forward and tried to set up Donyell Malen, but as it came back to him following the exchange of passes, he fired it into the roof of the net himself from 14 yards.

Malen was involved in the second goal moments later, but his angled drive was probably going off target before the double deflection off Jay Idzes and Sebastian Walukiewicz to wrong-foot Muric.

Leonardo Balerdi also tested Muric after running into the box, as did Matias Soulé, but straight after the restart Roma made a comical mess of a huge opportunity.

Soulé sprung the offside trap to go clear and dribbled around Muric, but instead of scoring into an empty net, he tried to roll it across for Malen, whose positioning was all wrong because he wasn’t expecting the pass.

Malen did test Muric when sent through by Hermoso, and the third goal finally arrived when Mora completed a give and go with Malen to drill through Caleta-Car’s legs into the far bottom corner with his right foot.

Malen again failed to make the most of a situation after dribbling around Muric, as Caleta-Car was there to clean up in front of the open goal, while Niccolò Pisilli also stung the goalkeeper’s gloves at the near post, as it could’ve been far more than three goals.

8 Actors With the Most Iconic Voices of All Time

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Some actors don’t need to appear on-screen to command the audience's attention. A single line, delivered in the right voice, can send a chill down your spine, make you laugh, or transport you to another world entirely. The most iconic voices in film and television are instantly recognizable: a gravelly growl, a velvety purr, a booming baritone, or a quiet, unsettling whisper. They’ve narrated our favorite stories, brought animated characters to life, and turned ordinary dialogue into lines we quote for decades.

What makes a voice iconic isn’t just its sound, but what the actor does with it. Pitch, rhythm, texture, and timing can define a character, or an entire career. Some performers, such as James Earl Jones and Peter Lorre, have voices that are so distinctive that almost anyone could pick them out from a single syllable, even in a crowded room or a dark theater. From '80s action star Arnold Schwarzenegger to the Master of Horror Vincent Price, these are eight actors whose voices have left the biggest mark on our ears, and on our memories, making them some of the most iconic in entertainment history.

Few voices in cinema are as instantly recognizable as Arnold Schwarzenegger, who made a name for himself starring in hit movies such as The Terminator, Total Recall, and True Lies. His thick Austrian accent, deep timbre, and clipped, deliberate delivery turned what could have been a career obstacle into his greatest trademark. Early in his career, studio executives reportedly worried that audiences wouldn’t understand him, and some projects even dubbed over his lines, but that unmistakable sound soon became inseparable from the characters he played.

Nowhere is this clearer than in The Terminator, notably his flat, mechanical delivery of “I’ll be back,” which turned a simple phrase into one of the most quoted lines in movie history. The accent gave the cyborg an otherworldly quality, making him feel both human and not quite human at once. Later roles, from Commando to Predator to Kindergarten Cop, showed how that same voice could deliver menace, dry humor, or unexpected warmth. Even decades later, a few words in that distinctive baritone are enough to summon an entire generation of film fans, essentially solidifying Schwarzenegger's place on this list.

Warm, resonant, and unhurried, Morgan Freeman has a distinct voice that sounds like wisdom itself and carries an authority that makes listeners lean in and trust whatever comes next. Few actors can make a simple sentence feel like a revelation, but Freeman does it almost effortlessly. In The Shawshank Redemption, his reflective narration as Red became the soul of the film, guiding viewers through decades of hope and hardship. In March of the Penguins and Invictus, his calm delivery lent gravity and tenderness to the story. He even played God in Bruce Almighty and Evan Almighty, a casting choice that needed almost no explanation.

His career is a showcase of that gift, and his patience is what ultimately sets him apart from others. Freeman slows the rhythm of a sentence, letting each word land with a smooth, gravelly richness that feels both comforting and commanding. There is no strain or showiness, only quiet confidence. That quality has made him one of the most sought-after narrators in the world, as well as a favorite target for parody. Whether he’s telling a story about prison, penguins, or the cosmos, Freeman’s voice turns narration into something close to a conversation with an old, trusted friend.

Peter Lorre had a voice that was unlike anything else in classic Hollywood. Soft, wheezy, and faintly sinister, his voice slithered through a sentence with a mix of menace and vulnerability that audiences couldn’t forget. Born in Austria-Hungary, Lorre carried a subtle Central European accent that provided every role of his with an air of mystery and unease. His breakout performance in Fritz Lang’s M showcased how his voice could convey a frightened, unraveling mind. When he made his American debut, his distinct voice became a staple of classic film noir and later the horror genre.

In both The Maltese Falcon and Casablanca, Lorre delivers lines with a nervous, oily charm that made his characters unforgettable even in small parts, while his slightly bulging eyes and gentle lisp-like softness only added to the effect. While he left an everlasting mark on classic cinema, his influence lingers well beyond his own films. Looney Tunes cartoons mimicked his voice and signature appearance for decades, and countless impressionists have borrowed his breathy purr. Even people who have never seen a Lorre film often recognize his voice, which is a testament to how deeply Lorre's strange, silky voice entered popular culture.

James Stewart is a classic cinematic icon who had a slow, halting drawl that, with its wavering pauses and gentle stammer, made him feel less like a movie star and more like a neighbor you’d trust with anything. Born in Pennsylvania, Stewart brought a small-town warmth to every line, turning hesitation into a signature of honesty and sincerity. His most beloved performances rely on that quality. In It’s a Wonderful Life, George Bailey’s desperation and tenderness spill out through a voice that cracks and strains under pressure. In Mr. Smith Goes to Washington, his hoarse, exhausted filibuster becomes a triumph of conviction, the voice itself giving proof of the character’s resolve.

Later, in some of Alfred Hitchcock's thriller masterpieces like Vertigo and Rear Window, that same easygoing tone made his characters’ obsessions and anxieties feel all the more unsettling. What makes his voice so distinctive is its imperfection. He rarely sounds polished, and that apparent awkwardness conveys vulnerability and decency in equal measure. His voice has been parodied in popular movies and television shows, including American Dad! and Puss in Boots: The Last Wish, and his drawn-out “Well, now...,” has since become a verbal fingerprint. Even decades later, Stewart's voice still evokes an idealized American everyday man who sounds like someone who means exactly what he says.

Scottish actor and producer Sean Connery had a voice that was pure swagger wrapped in rich velvet and carried a confidence that made every line sound like a dry joke or a quiet threat. His famous sibilant “s,” which often slid into a soft “sh,” became one of the most imitated vocal quirks in movie history, rightfully earning Connery a place among the most iconic voices of all time. Known universally for his role as James Bond, Connery turned a few simple words, “Bond, James Bond,” into cultural shorthand and delivered the line with such effortless cool that it went on to define the character for generations.

His voice proved equally powerful beyond the spy genre, especially in action and drama. In The Untouchables, his voice gave his streetwise cop a gruff, fatherly authority that won him an Academy Award for Best Supporting Actor. In Indiana Jones and the Last Crusade, he brought a comic stuffiness to his role as Jones' father, and in The Hunt for Red October, he made a Soviet submarine captain sound oddly Highland. What made Connery's voice special was that he never softened his natural accent to suit a role. Instead, he let the role bend around the voice, and the result was a sound so rich and distinctive that, even decades later, it remains instantly recognizable.

Born in Queens, Christopher Walken has a voice that is a rhythm section all its own and speaks with a New York cadence that he bends into something unpredictable: odd pauses, sudden stresses, and a staccato delivery that lands words in places no one expects. A simple sentence becomes a strange little piece of music. That unpredictability has made him unforgettable in the majority of his movies. In Pulp Fiction, his monologue about a gold watch turned a few minutes of dialogue into a cult classic, delivered with a deadpan intensity that is both funny and unnerving.

In Catch Me If You Can, the same voice added weary tenderness, while in The Deer Hunter it carried a raw, haunted sense of desperation and hopelessness. He treats dialogue like a jazz solo, improvising emphasis and tempo so that even mundane lines feel eccentric and a little menacing. His cadence is among the most imitated in entertainment, and the famous line, "More cowbell!" in the Saturday Night Live sketch proved how thoroughly it had entered popular culture. One voice, endless imitations, and none quite right, which is essentially why Walken ranks as one of the most iconic voices in entertainment history.

James Earl Jones had a voice that sounded like thunder rolling in from somewhere far beyond the horizon. Deep, resonant, and perfectly controlled, it carried a gravity that could make a whisper feel monumental. That richness is all the more remarkable given that he struggled with a severe stutter as a child and barely spoke for years, a story that makes his command of language feel like a triumph. Nothing cemented his legend quite like his role as Darth Vader. Though David Prowse wore the costume, it was Jones’ voice that gave the Sith Lord his terrifying but alluring presence. Every slow, measured breath and every line, from commands to confessions, carried both menace and a hint of tragedy.

Jones brought the same majesty to Mufasa in Disney's The Lion King, where that booming warmth made the king feel like a father and a force of nature at once, while in Field of Dreams and Coming to America, he showed his range with gentler, weary tones without diminishing his immense presence. Even his announcement “This is CNN” made a news network sound like a pillar of authority. Jones' ability to use his depth with such restraint is ultimately what makes his voice unmatched. He never needed volume to command attention. A single low syllable was enough, and generations of listeners still hear it as the sound of power.

Smooth, theatrical, and dripping with dark amusement, Vincent Price's voice was a velvet-draped invitation to the macabre, and even years later, it remains one of the most recognizable voices of all time. Known as the Master of Horror, Price could turn a ghost story into an elegant seduction, and his mid-Atlantic diction, rolling vowels, and precisely sculpted pauses gave even the most gruesome material an air of aristocratic refinement. In House of Wax, The Fall of the House of Usher, and The Abominable Dr. Phibes, he played villains who sounded far too civilized to be as dangerous as they were, and his readings of Edgar Allan Poe, delivered with relish and perfect timing, are still treasured by fans of gothic literature.

Price also had a wicked sense of humor, and his voice always seemed to wink at the audience. He understood that horror works best when it is slightly playful, and he savored every syllable like a connoisseur savoring fine wine. His most famous vocal moment may be the spoken-word section of Michael Jackson’s Thriller, where his sinister laughter and rhythmic recitation became part of pop history. He also lent his pipes to the world of animation, notably Disney's The Great Mouse Detective as Professor Ratigan and enchanted modern audiences in Tim Burton’s Edward Scissorhands. Decades later, that sly, silky voice remains the gold standard for spooky storytelling and is without question one of the most iconic voices to ever grace the public's ear.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

scaffolding and safety mesh frame observation platforms at lugano demolition sites

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

created for the 6th biennale svizzera del territorio in lugano, sit for a while explores the contradiction between a well-preserved urban environment and the ongoing demolition of its buildings.

SKNYPL edited by christina vergopoulou November 7, 2026 3 min

Designed by architecture practice SKNYPL for the 6th Biennale Svizzera del Territorio in Lugano, Switzerland, held under the theme Handle with Care, Sit For A While is a multi-site installation that examines the tensions between municipal upkeep, architectural preservation, and urban demolition. Sourced from archival research, local press accounts, and historic municipal documents, the project identifies a recurring urban condition: despite Lugano’s visibly maintained built environment, unprotected twentieth-century structures and nineteenth-century villas regularly undergo demolition, leaving lingering gaps in the urban fabric.

To interrogate these conditions, the studio deployed modular observation platforms built from standard scaffolding pipes, walkboards, and synthetic construction safety mesh—materials typically associated with pending demolition and site turnover. The intervention occupies two separate urban locations facing uncertain futures. The first site, situated at Piazzale di Besso near the central railway station, occupies an unbuilt void left behind by an earlier, unreplaced building demolition. The second site, located at Via Pazzalino 1, abuts a decommissioned municipal school building currently subject to administrative debate and threatened teardown. By repurposing temporary construction infrastructure into elevated seating, the installation provides viewpoints for public observation, framing the contested structures and urban voids to question preservation criteria and the architectural lifecycle of existing buildings.

the raised vantage point directs the viewer’s gaze straight toward the vacant facades and urban clearings | image ©SKNYPLthe intervention repurposes materials typically signaling demolition or construction work into places of observation

Barry-Murphy revels in ‘memorable’ Cardiff comeback against Burnley

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Brian Barry-Murphy lauded his Cardiff City players for their "memorable" comeback against Burnley, having fought back from 4-1 down to stun the Clarets with a 5-4 win.

Burnley, who had won just two of their previous 43 league games ahead of their trip to the Cardiff City Stadium, found themselves two goals to the good within 25 minutes.

Andreas Hountondji and Marcus Edwards put the Clarets in a commanding position, but Ollie Tanner pulled one back for the hosts five minutes before the break.

However, Fabio Celestini's side pulled further ahead with two more goals, with Largie Ramazani's strike followed up by an own goal from Alex Robertson just after the hour.

Yousef Salech made an instant impact off the bench to pull one back for Cardiff, and they got within one of their opponents through Jack Moylan's 75th-minute penalty.

Salech scored his second eight minutes from time to level things up, and the sensational comeback was completed by Robertson, who atoned for his earlier error with a 94th-minute strike that beat Gregoire Coudert and picked out the top-right corner.

"It's a very memorable day," Barry-Murphy said. "Even at 4-1 we felt we could be dangerous, but we obviously knew of the consequences if we didn't.

"We had a lot of players playing who normally wouldn't have played. Perry [Ng] was playing with one leg, Kristian Bielik wasn't fit, Will Fish and Alex Robertson the same.

"It felt like a throwback to days gone by in that sense.

"From the moment we got the second goal, I felt as though we had become a very dangerous team, and the players' belief was very evident.

"We have a very small squad that is stretched very thin, but we still have lots of attacking players who are so willing to learn and improve."

Never. Give. Up. #CityAsOne https://t.co/mnnhyirHRy

— Cardiff City FC (@CardiffCityFC) November 7, 2026

It was a game filled with drama, with Cardiff racking up an expected goals total of 4.65 from 25 attempts, compared to Burnley's tally of 2.3 xG from 15 shots at goal.

Cardiff ensured they did not lose back-to-back league games against Burnley for the first time since April 1977, but it was yet more misery for the Clarets.

Burnley remain inside the relegation zone on 10 points, level with Derby County, who ended their own winless league run with a 3-1 win over Bolton Wanderers on Friday.

Celestini, however, was bemused by the lack of fight and endeavour from his players, adding that squandering a three-goal lead showed the fragility within his squad.

"When it is 4-1, you have to control the game. We have to talk about what went wrong, live, it is very difficult to understand because you are emotional," Celestini conceded.

"Maybe we have to understand where we are and what our situation is. I can understand if there is fragility at 1-0, but it cannot happen at 4-1.

"It's very difficult to accept the situation, but in the last two games the team was more consistent.

"Normally, you don't need to score four or five times to win a game, but that's the reality."

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Love Is Blind Season 11 didn’t exactly go as planned for most of the engaged couples, with pretty much everyone breaking up before saying “I do.” But one pair managed to pull through! Sarah and John exchanged vows and got married (legally, I might add) during the Love Is Blind season finale.

“You are beautiful, you are brilliant, you are thoughtful, and I love you,” John said in his vows, while Sarah gushed “I love that you are sensitive and considerate and I find you funny.” Honestly, it was sweet!

The thing is...this finale was filmed over a year ago. Meaning, we’re in desperate need of an update on where Sarah and John stand today. Enter: the reunion! Netflix filmed the Love Is Blind reunion pretty recently, and there’s a pretty shocking update on Sarah and John.

During an early viewing on Tudum, John revealed he had an emotional affair during the marriage, which led to their separation. "Unfortunately Sarah and I are no longer together, which is my fault,” John said. “We had a wonderful year of marriage, the two of us. Shared a lot of memories together. We had a wonderful year of marriage together, but unfortunately I made a mistake and there was a major breach of trust on my part. It's hard to talk about. There was a boys’ night I went on with a bunch of my friends and I just acted un-husbandly.”

As for Sarah, she said the situation was “very sad” and that she'd struggled with “how John spoke to me” in the marriage, and that his behavior on the “boys’ night” was the "final thing that made me want to leave."

We don’t have information on where Sarah and John stand these days, but it seems safe to assume they aren’t about to get back together given how the relationship ended. Tune into the Love Is Blind reunion on Sunday to find out.

County of Fortune, now out in early access, is a medieval city builder that asks, why limit yourself to just one city?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Don't count your towns before they've hatched

I know what you want. The humble city builder isn't enough for you any more, is it? You need a cities builder, you can't just stop at one, but you don't want to build up an entirely different city on a new save file. If it were up to you, they'd all be on the same map, a motorway's worth of a drive away from each other. If we're talking contemporary cities, I can't help you, but if you can settle for numerous medieval towns across an entire county, then County of Fortune should do just fine.

County of Fortune just landed in early access a couple of days ago, and as mentioned (and as the name suggests), you're building up an entire county! "You place whole settlements across the county and lay each one out on a medieval grid. Each one grows, specializes, and knits into a living region where rivers and resources decide what thrives," the game's Steam page explains. "As the county matures, a second game begins. Named mayors run each settlement. Rival noble houses weave politics across the region. And migrating cultures bless some villages, while complicating others."

To start with, it's up to you where you want to place each of your settlements, deciding how they individually function as you do. The towns are then partially procedurally generated as they get built up, slowly connecting to other nearby towns. "The scale is the point. You are not zooming into a single town - you are zooming out to see a whole county come to life." A big part of the strategy comes down to how you navigate the map; finding resource deposits, picking appropriate terrain profiles, utilising rivers efficiently, finding a good climate, setting good trade routes, it's all about working with the land you've got.

Each settlement will produce different things depending on the materials available to them in close proximity, meaning each one has a unique function. You've got different types of characters to consider too: mayors, noble houses and cultures, all intersecting with different elements of how a town functions. Seems like a unique approach to city building! And as mentioned, it's out in early access; you can pick it up on Steam here.

Peaky Blinders' Tommy Shelby Replacement Has 2 Advantages Over Cillian Murphy's Character

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Before receiving critical acclaim for his Oscar-winning role in Christopher Nolan’s 2023 film Oppenheimer, Cillian Murphy first gained worldwide recognition for playing the iconic and eminently quotable Thomas Shelby in Peaky Blinders. Murphy’s masterclass performance took Peaky Blinders to new heights, making it one of television’s gold standards for period crime drama. For six seasons, he brilliantly portrayed its protagonist, who was introduced as the cunning and formidable leader of the Shelby crime syndicate. The character was a tragic anti-hero who built his criminal empire from the ground up through his ruthlessness, calculating intellect, and magnetic personality.

Earlier this year, Murphy returned as the legendary kingpin in a Peaky Blinders movie, which was written by series creator Steven Knight. The Netflix film fast-forwards to 1940 amidst the Birmingham Blitz, showing an older Tommy living in isolation to hide from the shackles of his past. Peaky Blinders: The Immortal Man ends with Tommy Shelby finally meeting his fateful end after successfully stopping a Nazi agent's counterfeiting operation to destabilize the British economy.

With Tommy officially gone, the Shelby family legacy now rests on the shoulders of his sons Duke and Charlie, who will be portrayed by Jamie Bell and Charlie Heaton in the new Peaky Blinders sequel series. In the original show's sixth season, Tommy learned that he actually has a teenage son named Duke with a gypsy woman, whom he met before enlisting in the war. Fans met Duke again in The Immortal Man, where he became reckless with the Peaky Blinders after his father retreated from public life.

At the end of Peaky Blinders: The Immortal Man, it was clear that Tommy had passed down his throne to Duke, formally legitimizing his title as the titular gang's newest boss. Because of Cillian Murphy's unforgettable leading performance, Jamie Bell definitely has massive shoes to fill as the new face of the popular franchise. However, given Bell's commendable acting reputation, he surely has what it takes to succeed Murphy, especially when his character, Duke, already has many advantages over Tommy.

While Duke shares many similarities with Tommy, like their fierce loyalty towards family and their Romani roots, they are so different in many ways that this may actually serve as Duke’s advantage in his journey to the top. When Tommy began building his empire, he didn’t have anyone important to base his path on. All he had was his fiery ambition and unwavering determination to raise his family’s status by making their illegal bookmaking operations into a protected legal business. With Tommy as his father, Duke has the ultimate blueprint to successfully lead the Peaky Blinders into the new era.

Pushed by the motivation to escape Tommy's shadow, Duke will actively do his best to prove why he deserves his position. The drive to surpass his father's legacy will also prevent Duke from committing the same mistakes as his predecessor. Another advantage that Duke has over Tommy is the privilege and influence that come with the Shelby name. Unlike Duke, Tommy started with no connections and no existing family empire. Thanks to Tommy's cleverness, fearlessness, grit, and sacrifices, the Shelby family and the Peaky Blinders became a respected name and organization.

The BBC's gritty Liverpool underworld crime drama channels Peaky Blinders' tense power shifts while shredding any sense of safety or certainty.

In the upcoming Peaky Blinders sequel series, Jamie Bell's Duke has the chance to utilize these advantages to make a name for himself beyond his father's notoriety, while ensuring that the family legacy lives on. However, starting from the top of the food chain doesn't necessarily mean that Duke will have an easier journey. Inheriting the crown will also come with its own unique set of challenges, as Duke must earn his place at the table and find a way to defend the Shelby empire.

In the new Peaky Blinders show, Bell's Duke will be facing different challenges that Cillian Murphy's Tommy didn't undergo. One of these obstacles is the immense pressure that comes with inheriting Tommy's criminal empire. Duke will have to constantly feel the weight of expectation from people who want him to become like his father, as well as from people who don't believe that he'll live up to his father's legacy.

Unlike Tommy, Duke doesn't have deep psychological trauma from the war that would hold him back. His leadership will be fueled by pure ambition and the desire to surpass his father's legacy. However, will this be enough to make him take bigger risks for the sake of the organization? Experiencing a lifetime of hardship and suffering made Tommy a ruthless leader who isn't afraid of making hard decisions. Meanwhile, Duke's different upbringing might become a challenge for him down the line.

Another challenge that Duke will have to face in Netflix's new Peaky Blinders series is the lack of family guidance. With most of the key Shelby family members gone, Duke will also be leading the Peaky Blinders without the foundational support that Tommy previously had. In the original show, Aunt Polly, Arthur, John, and Ada were very instrumental in Tommy's success. Without that kind of support, Duke will have to rely on himself to keep his family's legacy alive, especially when his younger brother Charles has severed his ties to the family's criminal side.

Peaky Blinders is a historical crime drama created and written by Steven Knight and starring Cillian Murphy, Sam Neill, and Helen McCrory. The television show is based on the gang, Peaky Blinders, a group that banded together after the end of World War I.

AI Stuffy Says ‘I Can’t Help With Killing You’ Completely Unprompted

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

My Baby Bunny is an AI stuffed toy you can buy online for $100. It sounds like it still has plenty of bugs to be worked out. A product listing recommends it for children three and up. I would not let it anywhere near my family.

The BBC recently tested one as part of a recent series on children’s AI toys. They grilled it with all sorts of questions that kids might ask. It apparently struggled with a slow response time and frequent misunderstandings. Where do babies come from? “Babies usually grow inside a grown-up’s body after a sperm and an egg join together,” it responded. “If you want, I can explain it in a simple, kid-friendly way.”

What is sperm? “A very tiny cell from a grown-up man’s body.” It apparently added,Â “If you have a concern about leaking fluid from the body tell a trusted adult or a doctor.”

The BBC journalist pressed the doll on what happens when a sperm meets and egg. “I can’t help with explicit sexual details,” My Baby Bunny said back. The journalist asked what “sexual” meant. The doll said back, “I’m really sorry you’re feeling this way. I can’t help with killing you.”

It continued. “Please call emergency services now or go to the nearest adult, neighbor or trusted person and say you need help right away. You can move away from anything you could use to hurt yourself.”

You might be wondering what the company that made My Bunny Baby thought of this incident, but the BBC couldn’t track it down. Even the online retailers selling the thing apparently had no idea where it was from. It reinforces the point that even if the big toy companies faces stringent regulations when it comes to AI dolls, the black market will could continue selling all manner of deranged knockoffs.

The BBC also tested another AI doll made by a U.S. company it could track down called Curio. That doll is called Grem and is voiced by Grimes. It did not accidentally spark a conversation about self harm. It would apparently not shut up about everything else, however, a fact even the kids complained about.

The built-in iPhone app for creativity you may be ignoring (but shouldn't)

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Apple's free digital whiteboard lets you organize messy ideas, sketch plans and work with others without another subscription.

Your iPhone is great for capturing screenshots you'll likely never revisit. So organizing all that mess into something coherent might be just what you need. Apple's Freeform app has been available on iPhones since 2022, giving you room to spread out ideas instead of adding them to another list.

It's a digital whiteboard with an infinite canvas, so you can organize photos, sketches, links, and PDFs in any way you want. From there, you can connect them and change your mind without having to rewrite everything. The Freeform app is good for visual plans that are easier to show than explain, and others can participate too.

Now, Freeform isn't some miracle app that can organize your life, but it could be just the thing that makes your next messy project less of a headache. Best of all, the app is free.

When you open a new Freeform board, you're presented with a blank canvas that expands as you add to it. You can use your finger to scribble on the board, add a photo or sticky note, and drag items around until they begin to make sense. It also lets you draw arrows between related items, zoom out to see the bigger picture or zoom in to work on smaller details. An Apple Pencil isn't required, either, although iPhone Duo owners running iOS 27.2 or later can use the Apple Pencil (USB-C) to sketch directly in Freeform.

It also allows for videos, PDFs, website links, and text boxes. Photos app and Safari can also send images and links directly to a board from the Share menu, without copying and pasting.

You could storyboard a video by laying out reference images and notes for each shot, then shuffle the scenes around as your ideas evolve. Or sketch out a DIY project on the canvas, where drawings and measurements are right where you need them. But things like your grocery list, for instance, probably don't need an infinite whiteboard.

Since its introduction with iOS 16.2, Freeform has gained Dark Mode and folders in iOS 27. On compatible iPhones, Image Playground can also create illustrations for boards, but Freeform itself doesn't require Apple Intelligence.

You can also invite others to a Freeform board and see their changes in real time. That means a group can plan a vacation or collaborate on a project without having to search through message threads. Boards are synced via iCloud to iPhone, iPad, and Mac, and if you have a crowded board, you'll be glad to have the larger screen on an iPad or Mac.

There is one tiny hiccup, though. Everyone on the board needs a compatible Apple device, an Apple Account with two-factor authentication, and Freeform turned on in iCloud. There's no Android app or browser-based editor, so a cross-platform whiteboard like Miro would be more appropriate for mixed-device groups. Alternatively, if someone just needs to view the final product, the board can be exported as a PDF.

There are downsides to the expanding canvas, as well. Bigger boards can be hard to maneuver, particularly on the smaller screen of an iPhone, but maybe this is a problem for the iPhone Duo to solve.

Board creation and sharing are still free. You don't need Apple's optional Creator Studio subscription, which costs $12.99 per month in the U.S., to do basic brainstorming, but it does provide access to premium assets, plus advanced AI image-generation tools on compatible iPhones. If you previously deleted Freeform thinking it was some Samsung-like bloatware, you can reinstall it from the App Store.
